Brazil, 2014

The election this mod is about

Dilma Rousseff entered 2014 as the first woman to hold Brazil's presidency, heir to twelve years of PT government and favorite for reelection. The economy was slowing, inflation had returned to everyday conversation, the June 2013 demonstrations were still politically unresolved and Petrobras was moving from symbol of national development toward scandal.

Aécio Neves offered the PSDB its best route back to the Planalto since 2002. Eduardo Campos tried to break the PT–PSDB cycle from the center-left. After Campos died in a plane crash on 13 August, Marina Silva replaced him and, for several weeks, appeared capable of replacing the entire structure of the election with him.

The first round on 5 October produced Dilma at 41.60%, Aécio at 33.55% and Marina at 21.32%. Three weeks later Dilma defeated Aécio by 51.64% to 48.36% — 54,501,118 votes to 51,041,155. The presidential result was close. The Congress elected alongside it was not simple at all.

What Coração Valente changes

The mod treats 2014 as contingent rather than a prologue whose ending everyone already knows. The player can preserve the historical contest, break the runoff entirely, produce a Marina presidency, return the PSDB to power or create results large enough to distort the congressional and state races around them.

The congressional figures in the endings are estimates, not claims that presidential vote share mechanically converts into seats. Brazil elects the Chamber proportionally and the Senate and governors in separate state races.

JUNE 2013The demonstrations remain in the room

Public transport protests expand into a national argument over services, representation, corruption and the political system.

13 AUG 2014Eduardo Campos dies

The PSB presidential candidate dies in a plane crash in Santos. Marina Silva is registered as his replacement nine days later.

5 OCT 2014The first round

Dilma leads; Aécio overtakes Marina for second place. The TSE mathematically identifies the runoff at 19:56 with 91% of valid votes counted.

26 OCT 201451.64 to 48.36

Dilma wins one of the tightest presidential runoffs of the democratic era, while a highly fragmented Congress waits for the victor.

`, reading:`

Further reading

A short shelf for the two stories sitting underneath the mod: June 2013 and the election that followed it.

Camila Jourdan — “2013 – uma dimensão ética libertária”

Jourdan reads 2013 from inside the cycle of protest and repression, treating the demonstrations as an experience with its own political and ethical meaning rather than as a preface to later elections.

André Singer — “Brasil, junho de 2013: classes e ideologias cruzadas”

One of the most useful early attempts to identify who was actually in the streets. Singer works through the survey evidence and argues that June crossed older middle-class discontent with a younger, lower-paid layer produced during the Lula years.

Marcos Nobre — Choque de democracia: Razões da revolta

Written while the demonstrations were still unfolding. Nobre interprets June through his argument about the closed, coalition-heavy political system he calls “pemedebismo.” It is useful precisely because it captures an argument being made in real time.

Wagner de Melo Romão — “#naovaitercopa: manifestações, Copa do Mundo e as eleições de 2014”

The cleanest bridge between June, the World Cup protests and the election year. Romão asks what survived from 2013 into 2014 and how the state’s response to demonstrations shaped the political atmosphere around the tournament.

Oswaldo Amaral & Pedro Floriano Ribeiro — “Por que Dilma de novo?”

An ESEB-based study of the 2014 vote. Its strongest finding is the importance of retrospective evaluation of Dilma’s government, a useful corrective to reading the election only as a map of regions or party machines.

Felipe Borba, Luciana Veiga & Flávia Martins — negative campaigning in 2014

Based on focus groups with voters exposed to real campaign advertising. Particularly useful for the stretch of the campaign in which Dilma, Aécio and Marina spent weeks testing how hard an attack could land before it came back on the attacker.

Mauro Paulino & Alessandro Janoni — the runoff’s socioeconomic split

A contemporary Datafolha reading of the second round. Their point is that the famous regional map also reflected class, income and education differences inside those regions.

`, sources:`

Data & sources

Votes, candidates and Congress

TSE — Votação e resultados, Eleições 2014 is the starting point for the official presidential result and state returns. The 22 August registration of Marina Silva and Beto Albuquerque fixes the legal chronology after Eduardo Campos's death.

For the legislature, the mod checks the Câmara's 2014 elected benches and the Senado's post-election party count.

Polling and the shape of the race

Datafolha's archived releases are the easiest way to follow the race changing week by week. The 8–9 September survey, for example, has Dilma at 36%, Marina at 33% and Aécio at 15%, with the Marina–Dilma runoff narrowing sharply from the previous week. Contemporary reporting from Folha, O Globo, Estadão and Veja was used to check debate nights, campaign reactions and the order in which scandals actually hit.

TSE — definitive second-round result

54,501,118 votes for Dilma Rousseff and 51,041,155 for Aécio Neves; 51.64% to 48.36% of valid votes.

Câmara dos Deputados — Bancada da Eleição de 2014

The elected party benches by state. This is the useful table when a presidential storyline runs into the much messier legislature elected on the same day.

Senado Federal — bancadas a partir de 2015

The Senate after the election: PMDB remained the largest caucus, followed by PT and PSDB, with PSB increasing its representation.

IBGE — GDP in 2014

The revised national-accounts series puts 2014 real GDP growth at 0.5%, with investment falling 4.2%. It is a better source for the year than campaign-era estimates that were later revised.

IBGE — IPCA closes 2014 at 6.41%

The price series behind the inflation argument that follows the campaign from Davos to the runoff.

IBGE — unemployment in 2014

The old PME series averaged 4.8% unemployment across its six metropolitan regions in 2014, the lowest annual average in that series.

Field schedule

`:'

'}`; } // Largest-remainder allocation gives integer ballots and preserves each UF total. function ballotRows(res){ return Object.entries(res.states).map(([uf,shares],i)=>{ const total=Math.round(CV_STATE_DATA[uf].valid),rows=Object.entries(shares).map(([candidate,p])=>({candidate,votes:Math.floor(total*p),fraction:total*p-Math.floor(total*p)})); let remaining=total-rows.reduce((n,c)=>n+c.votes,0);[...rows].sort((a,b)=>b.fraction-a.fraction).forEach(c=>{if(remaining>0){c.votes++;remaining--;}}); return {uf,total,index:i,rows}; }); } function countingSnapshot(count,step){ const national={},states={};let reported=0,total=0; count.ballots.forEach(st=>{ const delay=st.index%7,ratio=Math.max(0,Math.min(1,(step-delay)/(48-delay))); const rows=st.rows.map((c,i)=>({candidate:c.candidate,votes:step>=48?c.votes:Math.floor(c.votes*Math.pow(ratio,.72+((st.index*3+i*7)%13)/15))})); const votes=rows.reduce((n,c)=>n+c.votes,0);rows.forEach(c=>national[c.candidate]=(national[c.candidate]||0)+c.votes); rows.sort((a,b)=>b.votes-a.votes);states[st.uf]={rows,votes,total:st.total,called:votes>0&&rows[0].votes>(rows[1]?.votes||0)+(st.total-votes)}; reported+=votes;total+=st.total; }); return {states,national,reported,total,percent:100*reported/total,complete:step>=48}; } function stopCount(){if(electionCount?.timer)global.clearTimeout(electionCount.timer);if(electionCount)electionCount.timer=null;} function renderCount(){ const c=electionCount;if(!c||!c.root.isConnected)return;const snap=countingSnapshot(c,c.step);c.snapshot=snap; const rank=Object.entries(snap.national).sort((a,b)=>b[1]-a[1]); c.root.querySelector('#progress_text').textContent=`${snap.percent.toFixed(1)}% counted · ${fmt(snap.reported)} of ${fmt(snap.total)} valid votes`; c.root.querySelector('#cv2014-count-progress').value=snap.percent; c.root.querySelector('#top_bar_list').innerHTML=rank.map(([k,v])=>`
